### Page 2

2  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/
上週重要市場資訊
 澳洲 8 月就業人數勁增 3.95 萬人大幅優於預期，但勞動參與率上升至 67.1%，
推升失業率至 4.6%，呈現勞動力擴增但市場邊際降溫跡象。澳洲央行總裁布拉
克日前指出，失業率回升至5%有助於抑制頑固通膨。考量物價長期高於目標中
點，市場預期澳洲央行本週二將重啟緊縮，升息 1 碼至 4.60%，創 2011年底以
來新高。
 瑞士央行（SNB）上週四宣布連續第五次將關鍵政策利率維持在0%不變，符合
市場預期。儘管受油價推升使 8 月通膨微升至 0.8%，但仍處於目標區間，央行
預期中期通膨壓力溫和，現行極度寬鬆立場依然適當。決議公布後瑞郎兌美元跌
至 1.2077。相較全球主要央行相繼緊縮，瑞士央行維持超低利率，策略轉為視情
況調節匯市以穩定瑞郎。
 標普全球公布歐元區 9 月綜合 PMI 初值升至 53.1，創三年新高，製造與服務業
同步擴張，新訂單增速達四年多最快，印證經濟無懼美伊衝突引發的能源成本飆

升，展現超預期韌性。受能源價格傳導加速影響，通膨壓力再度升溫，儘管尚未
浮現薪資二輪效應，但強勁景氣數據促使市場大幅提高歐洲央行提前於10月例
會再度升息的押注。
 美國 9 月綜合 PMI 初值勁升至 58.4，創 62 個月新高；製造業與服務業 PMI 亦
逆勢走強至 57與 58.7，凸顯企業擴張強勁，引發市場對通膨持續性的擔憂。市
場焦點轉向 9 月 30日公布的美國 8 月 PCE、個人消費支出與 Q2 GDP終值，同
日日本亦將發布短觀調查與央行意見摘要。若終端消費延續強勁，將牽動美日央
行政策利率預期。

### Page 3

3  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
 美元兌新台幣匯率走勢  
Sep/29/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週台幣匯價高點在 31.655，低點在 31.866，以 31.790作收，較前一周收盤價
升值 2.8分。 
上週台幣先升後貶，周初，台股創新高，外資連續五日買超台股並匯入，推升
台幣最高來到 31.655，不過逢低吸引進口商、政府基金等買盤進場，台幣主要在
31.700 附近震盪；臨近周末，美國 9 月綜合 PMI 由 8 月的 56 走升至 58.4，創下逾
5 年來的新高，加上美伊戰事持續，皆推升市場對後續升息的預期，美元指數升至
101 上方，台幣跳空開低，回貶至 31.800 上方，最低來到 31.866，不過隨後出口商
賣壓出籠，台幣跌勢放緩，最終在央行調節之下收在 31.790。 
＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週台幣區間震盪。就國際市場而言，美國經濟表現仍具韌性，9月綜合
PMI 由 8 月的 56 上升至 58.4，企業活動加速至逾 5 年來最強，數據公布後美國 10
年債券殖利率升破 5%，Fed Watch 顯示市場對於聯準會 10 月升息預期升高，有助
於美元指數維持偏強格局，預計將給台幣帶來壓力；就台灣市場來看，台股在創下
新高後回落，而外資也結束連續五日買超的走勢，轉為賣超，短線上資金匯入不利
台幣表現，然而，台灣8 月外銷訂單達 1,029.6億美元、年增 71.4%，創歷史新高，
AI 需求帶動出口持續強勁，出口商後續拋匯需求預計將提供台幣支撐。綜合以上，
預估本週台幣區間震盪。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌台幣：31.600-32.000

### Page 4

4  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌人民幣匯率走勢  
Sep/29/2026 
＜上週走勢＞ 
上週人民幣震盪走貶。人民幣中間價在上周連續十天走升，並升破6.7500整數
關口，最高來到 6.7468，帶動人民幣偏升，不過由於美伊戰事持續，市場對於聯準
會升息預期提升，美元指數震盪走強，加上週間公布美國9月綜合 PMI由 8 月的 56
走升至 58.4，創下逾 5 年來的新高，美元指數突破 101 整數關卡，令境外人民幣承
壓，USD/CNH回貶至 6.7200上方，終場境外人民幣收在6.7227，週線貶值283點，
境內人民幣則收在 6.7125，週線貶值 141 點。

＜未來分析＞ 
     預估本週人民幣高檔震盪。就國際市場來看，美中兩國完成新一輪談判，美方
表示雙方同意將原訂 11 月 10 日到期的貿易休戰延長至明年 1 月 10 日，並持續就
關稅、貿易及供應鏈等議題進行協商，降低短期貿易摩擦升溫的風險，對人民幣形
成一定支撐。不過，美國近期經濟數據仍具韌性，加以聯準會偏鷹立場推升美元，
預計將限制人民幣升值空間；就中國國內市場來看，近期人行持續偏強設定中間價，
帶動人民幣維持偏強走勢，惟美中利差再度擴大，預計將限制人民幣升值的空間；
最後就技術面來看，USD/CNH日線圖中，匯價反彈至 20日均線之上，顯示原先下
行動能暫歇，上方壓力先看向季線 6.7350附近，綜合以上，預估本週人民幣高檔震
盪。

＜預估本週＞ 美元兌人民幣：6.6900-6.7400

### Page 5

5  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌日圓匯率走勢  
         Sep/29/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
上週 USD/JPY先上後下。週初，美國多位聯準會官員鷹派談話，帶動美元指數
持續上行，推升 USD/JPY延續前一週的走勢來到 157上方；週間，受美國 PMI 強
於預期與美債殖利率全面上行帶動，市場重新提高對聯準會後續升息路徑的押注，
帶動 USD/JPY大漲突破 159的關卡；臨近週末，日本首相高市早苗表態日圓被低估
是個問題，使市場提高對政策訊號與潛在干預的敏感度，帶動USD/JPY回吐大部分
漲幅，終場收在 157.26，週線上漲 56點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 USD/JPY區間震盪，主要原因如下：一、日本財務大臣片山皋月上週
五罕見透露，美國總統川普在美日元首峰會上對日圓過度疲弱表達關切，首相高市
早苗亦回應日圓遭低估確屬問題。儘管近期美元指數維持在 101 上方，但在日本政
府可能干預的陰影下，預計將限縮日圓貶值的空間。二、美國9 月綜合 PMI產出指
數創逾五年新高，疊加美伊戰事推升油價突破100美元與柴油大漲，通膨壓力劇增。
美聯儲理事巴爾表態可能需再升息兩次，芝加哥聯儲古爾斯比也示警能源衝擊具持
續性，推升市場押注 10 月升息機率升至六成，預計將支撐 USD/JPY 走勢。三、就
技術面而言，日線圖中，USD/JPY連日上漲一度站回年線但隨即失守，目前依舊承
壓於季線及年線下方，不過各天期均線趨平，且下方尚有月線支撐，短線尚無明確
的方向。綜合以上，預估 USD/JPY本週呈現區間震盪的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 美元兌日幣：155.30-159.30 
前週交易區間 
156.56 159.03

### Page 6

6  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
美元兌歐元匯率走勢  
             Sep/29/2026

＜上週走勢＞ 
  上週 EUR/USD 下跌。週初，歐元延續前一週偏弱的走勢，加上聯準會官員鷹
派發言，導致歐元跌破 1.1450的重要關卡；週間，儘管油價自高檔回落，但市場解
讀為能源成本仍處高位、難以迅速回落，推升對美國實質利率與利差優勢的預期。
加上歐洲在能源依賴與成長動能較弱下，相對於美國更難承受長期高利率環境，歐
元最低來到 1.1358；臨近週末，歐元跌深反彈，終場收在1.1391，週線下跌 91點。 
＜未來分析＞ 
  預估本週 EUR/USD 震盪偏弱，主要原因如下：一、歐洲央行首席經濟學家連
恩示警，第二波能源價格飆升將推升通膨，預計至2027年中期始回落至目標水準。
德國央行月報指出，受中東衝突、低天然氣庫存及明年醫療改革影響，通膨將居高
不下；夏季雖因出口疲弱與乾旱致增長趨緩，但在製造業回暖與財政支出挹注下，
估年底經濟有望重啟復甦，預計將提供歐元一定的支撐。二、美聯儲官員接連釋放
強烈鷹派信號，聖路易聯儲總裁穆薩萊姆直言當前 3.75%-4.00%利率仍偏寬鬆，呼
籲盡早收緊；芝加哥聯儲總裁古爾斯比亦警告，AI投資繁榮引發需求過熱，疊加持
續性供應衝擊，可能迫使美聯儲加速且更積極地升息。目前Fedwatch顯示預期明年
4 月前可能再升息三次，將不利歐元表現。三、技術面而言，在日線圖中，EUR/USD
在各天期均線下方，且接連失守1.15及 1.14整數關卡，空方動能較佔優勢。綜合以
上，預估 EUR/USD本週呈現震盪偏弱的可能性較高。 
＜預估本週＞ 歐元兌美元：1.1300-1.1450 
前週交易區間 
1.1358 1.1495

7  兆豐國際商業銀行 http://www.megabank.com.tw/ 
                           新台幣利率走勢分析

115.09.21-115.09.24 
銀行隔夜拆款利率 0.804% ~ 0.830%  
30天期短票利率   1.65% ~ 1.69%  
公債－10 年期-A15109     1.95%  
－ 5 年期- A15108 1.84%

一、 台幣貨幣市場部分，本周央行持續每日減發定存單，令市場資金充裕，帶動短
率靠向區間低位。 
週二央行標售 2 年期定存單 250 億元，加權平均得標利率為 1.638%，略低於
市場預期，且較上次標售結果下跌 2.5個基本點。 
週四隔夜拆款利率區間在 0.804%~0.830%， 30 天期短票主要成交在
1.65%~1.69%，利率較上週下跌 1 個基本點。 
二、 本週市場交投冷清，10 年期指標券 A15109 及 5 年期指標券 A15108 均未成
交，週四等殖系統參考報價中價分別為 1.95%、1.84%。 
台股排擠效應、國際債市走勢偏空，加上油價續處高檔，通膨潛在威脅仍存，
12 月升息可能性未能完全排除，市場氣氛續偏觀望，預期台債殖利率將維持
高檔區間盤整 ， 預估5年券區間為 1.84%~1.90%，10年券區間為 1.95%~2.0%。

### Page 1

Company Report 
公司研究報告 
本報告僅供宏遠投顧內部及客戶參考，雖已力求正確與完整，但因時間及市場客觀因素改變所造成產業、市場
或個股之相關條件改變，投資人需自行考量投資之實際狀況與風險，本公司恕不負擔任何法律責任及做任何保
證。非經本公司同意，不得將本報告加以引用或轉載

1 
2408 南亞科(Nanya Technology)

報告日期： 2026/10/13 研 究 員：翁浩軒  (Davy Wong)  
壹、訪 談／法 說 內 容 
重點摘要： 
 3Q26 毛利率優於預期且將維持 8 成以上 
 2027 年 DRAM 供需仍緊俏 
 新廠 2027 年第四季加入貢獻

一. 3Q26 毛利率優於預期，EPS 25.4 元 
南亞科 3Q26 營收 1336.49 億(QoQ+61.9% 、YoY+611.7%) ，其中位元
出貨量季減 4~6% ， 平均銷售單價季增超過 60%(主要因反應長期合
約重新簽訂之調整)，匯率影響有利 1~3%，3Q26 毛利率上升至
85.7%，主要因平均銷售單價上升，營業費用季增 28 億元，因當
期及未來的員工薪酬增加，稅後淨利 876.27 億(QoQ+74.58%、
YoY+5505.16%)，EPS 25.4 元。

二. 4Q26 整體產業仍將維持供不應求態勢  
受惠於 AI 需求 ， AI 運算（GPU 、 TPU 、 CPU 及 ASIC）成為 DRAM 
需求成長主力，帶動高容量、高頻寬及客製化記憶體架構需求，
範疇從 HBM 、LPDDR5 進一步擴展至網路、儲存與系統管理等領
域。在傳統應用市場方面，由於 DRAM 供給不足與 ASP 上漲，
部分傳統應用終端出現產品降規或延長生命週期的現象，低階市
場則有回收 DRAM 支撐部分需求。供給端方面，因新廠建置、設
備交期、製程導入均需要較長的前置時間，目前供給仍維持緊俏，
智慧型手機、個人電腦及一般消費性記憶體供給受限，製造商與
供應鏈端的庫存水位普遍偏低 ， 隨著供應商依 LTA 長約進行產品
組合與產能規劃，有利未來供需穩定度。

三. 新廠預估 4Q27 開始投產 
公司 5A 新廠預計 2027 年第一季裝機，2027 年第四季開始投
產 ， 預計 2028 年底前達到每月投片量 3 萬片 ， 全產能目標於 2030 
年底前達每月 5 萬片，未來產能擴充均以客戶長期合約的 AI 專
案為主要目標。另外公司亦佈局先進封測領域，規劃於屏東科學
園區建置 3D 晶圓封裝與測試廠 ， 預估 2H28 設備進駐 。 公司 2026
年前三季累計資本支出為 276 億元 ， 2026 年資本支出以不超過 697
億元為上限，其中生產設備資本支出約占五成。

四.  2026 / 2027 年獲利創高，預估 EPS 77.1 元/ 134.3 元

展望 2026 下半年至 2027 年，AI 需求維持強勁，AI 及一般雲端伺

憶體需求持續上揚。供給方面，2026 年產業微幅增加產能，預估
2027 年新增產能有限，多種 DRAM 產品將持續缺貨，三大原廠新
增產能實際貢獻要到 2028 年才會顯現 。 南亞科新廠預計 2028 年放
量，初期每月投片量 3 萬片，全產能將達 5 萬片/月。

四.投資建議：買進，目標價 670 元 
南亞科為全球前五大 DRAM 廠，受惠於 AI 帶動之供給端改善，記
憶體產品漲價快速，南亞科今明年獲利創高，2028 年全球雖然有
新產能開出，但隨著 AI 由模型訓練與推理延伸至更多客製化邊緣
AI 應用，帶動記憶體位元需求長期成長。考量到 AI 應用滲透率提
升帶動記憶體需求，記憶體已成戰略物資，客戶亦開始簽訂長約，
參考 2027 年 EPS 給予 5 倍本益比，目標價 670 元，建議買進

貳、產 業 及 營 運 概 況

一.公司簡介 
南亞科成立於 1 995 年，為 DRAM （動態隨機存取記憶體）之研
發、設計、製造與銷售商，為全球第四大 DRAM 廠，市佔率約 
3%，由美光授權製程技術，其十二吋晶圓廠位於泰山南林園區，
月產能 6.8 萬片 ， 其中 20nm 製程約 3.8 萬片 ， 30nm 製程約 3 萬
片，公司產品廣泛應用於手機、PC、消費性電子產品，主要競爭
對手包括 SK Hynix、三星電子、美光、華邦電等。

二.產業趨勢概況  
1. AI 帶動記憶體價格雙位數上漲 
全球 CSP 大廠持續加強投資 AI server 及相關基礎建設，全球九大
CSP 廠之資本支出持續高速成長，2026 年資本支出將達 9220 億元
(YoY+98%)，2027 年資本支出成長至 13830 億美元(YoY+50%)。
TrendForce 預估 2026 年 HBM 需求量年增 70%。而隨著 AI 應用由
LLM 模型訓練延伸至推論，CSP 廠擴大通用型伺服器布建，也使
記憶體拉貨重心由 HBM 延伸至 RDIMM ， 進一步推升 DDR5 價格。
3Q26 在 AI 與資料中心建置需求持續下記憶體仍嚴重供不應求 ， 儘
管價格基期已高，但預估 DRAM 合約價持續上漲，TrendForce 預
估第三季整體 conventional DRAM 合約價上漲 13-18% ， 第四季持續
上漲 10%~15%。供給方面，DRAM 三大原廠今年新增產能有限，
三星及海力士均以既有廠區調整及製程轉換為主，預估晶圓產能
分別增加 5%及 8%，而美光產能持平，三大原廠新廠產能開出並

3 
明顯貢獻預估在 2028 年 。 預估 2026 年 DRAM 價格逐季調升 ， 2027
年新增供給有限下，價格易漲難跌。

2. 2027 年記憶體需求維持強勁 
展望 2026 第四季至 2027 年，AI 需求維持強勁，AI 及一般雲端伺
服器 DRAM 需求持續擴大，接下來逐步擴散至非雲端市場 (如: 
Edge、客製化)，小型語言模型推廣 AI 至邊緣裝置 (PC、手機、汽
車、機器人等)，新增客製化 DRAM 需求，整體記憶體需求持續
上揚。需求面依各應用別而言，Server 方面，AI 及高效能運算持
續驅動 HBM 與 DDR5、LPDDR5 需求，高階 e-SSD、BMC、NIC
對 DDR4、LPDDR4 及 DDR5 需求顯著; PC 及手機方面，高階 PC
與手機對 DDR5 及 LPDDR5 需求維持穩定 ， 然中低階需求疲弱; 消
費型電子市場方面，AI 及一般伺服器對 HBM 、RDIMM 需求持續
擴張，排擠一般消費型記憶體供給。供給方面，2026 年產業微幅
增加產能，聚焦於高階雲端與高附加價值產品需求，DRAM 大廠
優先分配產能於 DDR5 及 HBM 產品 ， 預估 2027 年新增產能有限，
多種 DRAM 產品將持續缺貨，三大原廠新增產能實際貢獻要到
2028 年才會顯現 。 南亞科第二代 10 奈米級製程(1B) 16Gb DDR5 產
品交運量已達 10%，第三代 10 奈米級(1C)及第四代 10 奈米級(1D)
製程技術試產中。公司 DDR5 營收約占 10%，並可視需求彈性調
整增加，同時持續供應 DDR4 及 LPDDR4 產品以緩解市場缺口。
公司亦積極與策略夥伴合作拓展 AI 邊緣運算驅動的記憶體市場，
目前處工程驗證階段，預計於 2026 年底推出新產品。南亞科 2025
年資本支出 134 億元，2026 年資本支出預計不超過 697 億元，其
中生產設備支出約占五成，新廠預計於 2027 年第一季開始裝機，
2027 年第四季開始生產，2028 年放量，初期每月投片量 3 萬片，
預估 2030 年全產能將達 5 萬片/月。南亞科 2025 年位元銷售量成
長 50 多%，預估 2026 年成長 17~19%，預估 2027 年成長中低個位
數。

叁、業 績 及 獲 利 預 估

1、3Q26 毛利率優於預期，EPS 25.4 元 
南亞科 3Q26 營收 1336.49 億(QoQ+61.9% 、YoY+611.7%) ，其中位元
出貨量季減 4~6% ， 平均銷售單價季增超過 60%(主要因反應長期合
約重新簽訂之調整)，匯率影響有利 1~3%，3Q26 毛利率上升至
85.7%，主要因平均銷售單價上升，營業費用季增 28 億元，因當
期及未來的員工薪酬增加，稅後淨利 876.27 億(QoQ+74.58%、
YoY+5505.16%)，EPS 25.4 元。

2、2026/2027 年營收獲利創高，預估 EPS 77.1 / 134.3 元

during Aug, resulting in 3Q26 revenues up 74% YoY to NT$843m. With the gradual ramp 
up of clients’ new platform, we expect to see sequential MoM growth ahead, with Oct/

Earnings revision: We factor in UMT 3Q26 revenues and revise down earnings by 1%/ 
1%/ 1% in 2026-28E mainly on lower revenues due to the momentum of product 
delivery, while we remain positive on the rising LEO satellite business exposure ahead.

Valuation: We continue to derive our target price based on a discounted P/E, and based 
on 2029E (methodology unchanged). We continue to derive the target P/E multiple from 
the average PEG&M ratio of peers in the satellite supply chain, and we remove outliers 
(telecom operators who face competition from LEO satellite operators). Our target 
2029E discounted P/E multiple is at 37.1x (vs. previously at 37.0x) which is based on 
peers’ avg. PEG&M, and UMT’s 2029-30E avg. NI YoY and OPM, due to higher avg. OPM 
in 2029-30E at 42% (vs. previously at 41%). We apply the 37.1x multiple to 2029E EPS 
and discount it back to 2027E via a COE of 7.8% (no change). Our 12m target price is at 
NT$2,549 (vs. previously at NT$2,557), implying 61x 2027E P/E, which is above the 
company’s average +1 stv. P/E of 40x, reflecting our positive view on UMT’s product mix 
upgrade toward LEO satellites. Maintain Buy.

### Page 4

Price target risks and methodology - UMT 
Valuation: We use a discounted P/E methodology and apply a 37.1x target P/E multiple 
to UMT’s 2029E EPS, discounting it back to 2027E at a COE of 7.8% (beta 1.2x, risk-free 
rate 1.6% and market risk premium at 5.1%), which leads to our 12-month target price 
of NT$2,549. The target P/E is based on the average PEG&M ratio of peers in the 
satellite supply chain. We are Buy-rated on UMT.  
Key Risks: Slower-than-expected LEO satellite deployment; Potential competition from 
new-entrant suppliers; LEO satellite operators manufacturing in-house components.

Source: Company data, Goldman Sachs Research estimates, FactSet. Price as of 12 Oct 2026 close.

Buy (B), Neutral (N), Sell (S) Analysts recommend stocks as Buys or Sells for inclusion on various regional Investment Lists. Being assigned a Buy or Sell
on an Investment List is determined by a stock’s total return potential relative to its coverage universe. Any stock not assigned as a Buy or a Sell on an
Investment List with an active rating (i.e., a  stock that is not Rating Suspended, Not Rated, Early-Stage Biotech, Coverage Suspended or Not Covered), is

deemed Neutral. Each region manages Regional Conviction Lists, which are selected from Buy rated stocks on the respective region’s Investment Lists
and represent investment recommendations focused on the size of the total return potential and/or the likelihood of the realization of the return across
their respective areas of coverage.  The addition or removal of stocks from such Conviction Lists are managed by the Investment Review Committee or
other designated committee in each respective region and do not represent a change in the analysts’ investment rating for such stocks.

Total return potential represents the upside or downside differential between the current share price and the price target, including all paid or
anticipated dividends, expected during the time horizon associated with the price target. Price targets are required for all covered stocks. The total
return potential, price target and associated time horizon are stated in each report adding or reiterating an Investment List membership.

Document 110: 261015_daiwa_TSMC

Source PDF: all report pdf/261015_daiwa_TSMC.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

Taiwan Information Technology

What’s new: TSMC continued delivering “beat-n-raise” prints for its 
quarterly results and guidance on 15 October. While we raise our forecasts 
to factor in its better topline and pricing trajectory yet muted margin 
expectation on an incremental basis, we see likely lack of near-term 
passion from investors on limited positive surprises from its results call this 
time. That said, we believe AI demand remains strong and sustainable to 
support our Buy (1) call on the stock, though patience may be necessary 
for investors amid the current jittery environment.

What’s the impact: Results intact, guidance mixed. Despite 3Q26 
topline of USD46.7bn topping guidance (USD44.6-45.8bn) at TSMC, its 
3Q26 EPS came in at TWD29.4, only marginally beating our/Bloomberg 
consensus estimates on lower opex and higher non-operating gains from 
long-term investments more than offsetting lower gross margin associated 
with higher depreciation costs on dilutive N2 ramp (table on p.2). Guidance 
calls for higher topline for 4Q26 with midpoint of the range 6% above our 
previous forecast, thanks to continued AI strength as customers continue to 
increase demand. Yet margins at the operating level looked muted without 
further operating leverage being expected by the street, due to the margin-
dilutive N2 ramp. We raise our 2026-28E forecasts by 3-10% to factor in 
the better topline trajectory, plus our view of better pricing power.

Lack of near-term passion? Apart from the upbeat topline but muted 
margin guidance on an incremental basis, we left the results call with 
limited positive surprises this time which may not drive any passion for 
investors in the near term to catalyse the stock. That said, fundamentals 
remain strong since AI demand continues to mushroom from accelerators 
to CPU and other companion chips, as language models transform from 
generative to agentic for AI assistance. TSMC implied upside risk to its 
long-term AI accelerator revenue CAGR of mid-to-high-50% over 2024-29 
and, as flagged in our Asian Foundry report, we saw upside risk to TSMC’s 
pricing power for 2027, given the still meaningful supply constraint where 
management reiterated its strong conviction in this multi-year AI megatrend 
and active capacity build aimed to narrow the supply/demand gap.

What we recommend: We raise our 12M TP for TSMC to TWD3,200 
(previous: TWD3,000), based on our finetuned ROE-adjusted PBR target of 
7.5x one-year forward BVPS forecast (previous: 7.7x). We reiterate our Buy 
(1) call with key downside risks of “silicon inflation” and geopolitical 
overhangs leading to global inflation and AI capex cut.

How we differ: Our 2028E EPS is 11% below the Bloomberg consensus 
likely on our view calling for a soft-landing in the chip cycle.

15 October 2026

Lack of passion despite strong AI?

➢ Another “beat-n-raise” in quarterly prints but likely … 
➢ … short of catalysts on limited positive surprise this time 
➢ Reaffirming our Buy (1) call with a higher 12M TP of TWD3,200

Company profile 
Incorporated in Taiwan in 1987, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co. (TSMC) is the world’s largest 
complementary metal oxide semiconductor (CMOS) foundry in revenue terms. TSMC offers foundry 
services such as wafer masking, fabrication, probing, packaging and testing, to a high variety of 
customers including fabless chipmakers and IDMs. Its manufacturing fabs are located in Taiwan, China, 
the US and Singapore.

### Page 5

5

Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (2330 TT): 15 October 2026 
ESG analysis

Update Date: 15 Oct 2026 
Source: Daiwa, Company

ESG risks
Risks Management Analyst comments
G
Executive/board
quality 1
TSMC's board of directors (BoD) has 10 members, including 6 independent directors
representing 60% of total directors. The background of TSMC's BoD is diversified, with members
from industries, academia, and law. Also, its board members include representatives from not
only Taiwan but also Europe and the US, bringing their experience in operating international
businesses to the board.
We believe the high percentage of independent directors and a diverse mix of expertise from the
board members strengthen the functionality of its BoD, as evidenced by TSMC's leading position
in the foundry market.
Capital management 1
TSMC pays its dividend on a quarterly basis. In the past 5 years, it has managed its dividend
payout well, as evidenced by its dividend in dollar terms, where it has never lowered its dividend.
As the leader in advanced technology nodes, TSMC has kept expanding its capacity for
advanced nodes; as such, we expect its capital intensity to increase to the level near or above
40% over 2025-26 (vs. its long-term target of mid-30%). However, we believe TSMC will at least
sustain its cash dividend payout in dollar terms over 2025-26E. We see its balance between
investment and dividend as appropriate.
Related party &
transaction 1
TSMC's sales to related parties and purchases from related parties remain immaterial as a
percentage of total revenue and cost of goods sold in 2026. We view these levels as insignificant
and therefore see limited risk to TSMC's earnings, cash flow generation, or corporate
governance.
S Supply chainmanagement 1
As the leader in the global foundry industry, TSMC approaches supply chain management by
focusing on 2 main strategies: sustainability risk management and local supply chain
optimisation. It requires all its suppliers to comply with its "Code with Ethics and Business
Conduct", and to follow regulations on human rights and conflict-free minerals.
In 2019, 100% of its tier-1 suppliers signed the "Supplier Code of Conduct" and self-assessment
questionnaire on sustainable management. Also, TSMC's raw materials purchased are 100%
Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC) conflict-free. It also requires its critical suppliers to
report on the status of sustainability management in their critical upstream supply chains.
Through these measures, we see TSMC reducing its supply chain risk.
E Water & wastewatermanagement 2
TSMC applies internal and external company resources to develop water reclaimation
technology, implements water conservation measures and uses reeclaimed water in the
manufacturing process. For example, on September 19, 2022, TSMC Tainan Science Park
Reclaimed Water Plant started supplying reclaimed water. In 2025, TSMC aims to replace 5% of
water resources with reclaimed water, and continue to cooperate with the government to
complete the 2nd water reclamation plan in Anping, Tainan. In 2030, TSMC targets to replace >
60% of water resources with reclaimed water.
E
Waste & hazardous
materials
management
1
TSMC's waste recycling rate reached 96% in 2022 for 8 years in a row. Also, the percentage of
waste sent to landfills was less than 1% for 13 years in a row in 2022.
TSMC achieved reducing its outsourced unit waste disposal (kg/12" equivalent wafer mask
layer) to 0.99 in 2022. Furthermore, TSMC has set a goal to reduce its outsourced unit waste
disposal per wafer to no more than 0.5 in 2030.
E GHG emissions 1
TSMC has continued its efforts to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions to drive low-carbon
manufacturing. In 2022, TSMC achieved net zero emissions of direct and indirect (from
purchased energy) GHG Emissions in overseas factories, and 100% use of
carbon-neutral natural gas in Taiwan fabs. It has set a target to achieve Net Zero Emissions by
2050.
Note: Management score represents a company's ability to manage/benefit from certain ESG topics. The scores range from 1 to 3, with 1 being the strongest.

Greater China Technology Hardware  | Asia Pacific
Takeaways on Photonics from
2026 OCP Global Summit
Morgan Stanley Asia Limited+
Andy Meng, CFA

Andy.Meng@morganstanley.com                                                +852 2239-7689
Betty Chen

Betty.H.Chen@morganstanley.com                                                +852 2239-7213
Greater China Technology Hardware
Asia Pacific
Industry View In-Line

On Day 3 of the 2026 OCP Global Summit, there is a full-day special focus on
Photonics. Industry participants such as NVIDIA, Meta, Microsoft, Oracle, Broadcom,
Celestica, Arista, Ciena, Amphenol, Lumentum, and Huawei presented their views.
We summarize the key findings below:
1) When the moderator asks key industry players whether they will apply CPO or
NPO, almost all companies answered 'Both'.
2) AI density depends on optical connectivity. With the day's focus on Photonics, the

importance of optical connectivity was well highlighted in each session.
3) Five key metrics for new optical technology: 1) performance, 2) power, 3) cost, 4)
speed to deployment and 5) reliability and serviceability. Huawei believes NPO has
advantages on maintainability, open decoupling, availability and industry
implementation.
4) Scale-in is another new approach to enhance AI density.
5) Root cause of CPO failures: 83-85% of failures due to ELS, 11-17% due to fiber/

connector. Dust and dirt are also common failure issues.
6) Optics vs. Copper: Full Optical Pod is targeted towards scale-up sizes >576
accelerators, Optics and Copper to coexist between 72 and 576 accelerators.
Greater China Technology Hardware: Takeaways on NPO from 2026 OCP Global
Summit (14 Oct 2026)
Morgan Stanley does and seeks to do business with
companies covered in Morgan Stanley Research. As a result,
investors should be aware that the firm may have a conflict of

communications with a subject company, public appearances
and trading securities held by a research analyst account.
Key Takeaways
A full day discussion of Photonics further demonstrated the importance of
optical connectivity.

STOCK RATINGS
Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy,
Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell.  Investors should carefully read the definitions of all
ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision
to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -
relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.

Document 240: Business Checkpoint_ Looking into the logistics Profitability growth was positive in the third quarter, but logistics costs and supply chain pressures are creating challenges. 2 mi

Source PDF: all report pdf/Business Checkpoint_ Looking into the logistics Profitability growth was positive in the third quarter, but logistics costs and supply chain pressures are creating challenges. 2 min read.pdf

Economy 
Business Checkpoint: Looking into the logistics 
15 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Small and mid-sized businesses entered Q3 in relatively solid financial condition, with profitability growth up across both 
groups, according to Bank of America business account data. Nearly half of estimated tariff payments had been refunded by 
September, though the share of these refunds relative to revenue has peaked. 
• In Bank of America business payments data, logistics spending growth per client was up four times as much for small than mid-
sized firms. Additionally, distribution and inventory transactions growth per client has outpaced spending since the spring of 
2025. 
• Growing transportation bottlenecks are re-emerging ahead of the holiday season. Trucking capacity has tightened as driver 
availability remains constrained, according to BofA Global Research. This has pushed freight rates higher and driven strong 
transportation sector payroll growth, particularly among mid-sized businesses, according to Bank of America payments data.

Business Checkpoint is a new, regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It provides a real-time view of the financial health, opportunities 
and challenges facing small and mid-sized businesses across the US economy, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America's proprietary 
data. By analyzing activity among both small and mid-sized firms, Business Checkpoint offers timely insights into business profitability, hiring, 
investment and spending trends. Such data is not intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, 
financial condition or performance of Bank of America. 
Businesses end Q3 in fair shape 
While profitability growth remained positive for both small and mid-sized firms in the third quarter, small firms outpaced their 
mid-sized counterparts for the second consecutive quarter (Exhibit 2). Still, there’s been a notable reversal in the profitability of 
mid-sized businesses, growing this quarter after declining slightly for the past two.  
The continued rollout of tariff refunds is serving as a tailwind to some firms' cash positions. That is particularly true for small 
firms. According to Bank of America account data, by September approximately 47% of estimated International Emergency 
Economic Powers Act-related tariff payments had been refunded to companies with less than $50 million in annual revenue.

According to Bank of America business payments data, the percentage point difference in tariff refunds as a share of revenue 
for firms with less than $50 million in annual revenue spiked in June but dropped to nearly zero in September. At the same time, 
the percentage point difference in tariff payments as a share of expenses began rising again relative to the 2023 average 
(Exhibit 1). This suggests that while tariffs continue to affect companies, the refund program has provided temporary relief for 
some businesses.  
Wholesale trade along with transportation and warehousing posted solid profitability growth across business size 
However, the benefits appear uneven. Such refunds have boosted cash flow, but they have not fully insulated firms from broader 
cost pressures.  
Looking across industries, mid-sized construction and wholesale firms had the strongest profitability growth in September 
(Exhibit 3). Notably, transportation and warehousing, as well as wholesale trade were among the few sectors showing positive 
profitability growth for both small and mid-sized firms last month. 
The persistence of logistics, freight and inventory-related cost pressures may help explain why profitability gains have remained 
relatively concentrated in transportation and wholesale trade. However, these sectors also have a higher exposure to rising gas 
prices within their margins (read more on this in the August Small Business Checkpoint). 
Exhibit 3: For both small and mid-sized businesses, profitability growth was positive in transportation and warehousing as well as wholesale trade 
in September 
Profitability growth by sector and business size in September (YoY%, six-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The cost of maintaining supply chains is rising 
In fact, the prices paid component of the September services reading from the Institute for Supply Management indicates still-
building price pressure for firms (Exhibit 4). This marked the highest reading since July 2022.   
Using Bank of America internal data, we identified payments to logistics and freight companies to assess a key part of supply 
chain costs. In June, logistics and freight payments’ growth per client from small businesses grew nearly 30%, though has 
moderated to 12.7% YoY in September (Exhibit 5). Meanwhile, mid-sized firms’ payments growth in this category was up just 
3.2% YoY in September. 
The divergence suggests logistics costs may be disproportionately affecting smaller firms, who are typically less capable of 
absorbing rising costs than larger firms. Larger companies also often benefit from dedicated logistics contracts and greater 
purchasing power, while smaller ones may be more exposed to spot-market transportation costs and fluctuations in freight 
pricing.

Evidence of more moving parts in companies’ supply chains 
Yet there is increasing evidence that tariffs and geopolitics are adding complexity to small and mid-sized companies’ supply 
chains. 
In Bank of America payments data, average distribution and inventory transaction growth per business client has increased 
steadily since the spring of 2025 despite spending growth in this category falling in September (Exhibit 6). Rising transaction 
growth alongside softer spending growth suggests businesses may be engaging with more suppliers, shipments or inventory 
movements even as overall procurement outlays moderates.  
Furthermore, in May 2026, the Global Supply Chain Pressure Index reached its highest reading since the summer of 2022 and has 
continued to remain elevated into October (Exhibit 7). This further emphasizes that transportation bottlenecks, delays and 
procurement challenges are becoming more common globally. While current readings remain below the extreme levels observed 
during the pandemic, the recent move higher indicates supply chain normalization may be reversing.

The trials of trucking  
As of October 1, BofA Global Research's proprietary bi-weekly Truckload Demand Indicator (see Methodology) remained below 
its historical growth signal (Exhibit 9) and the Truck Capacity Indicator, which reflects shippers' views of available capacity, 
tightened significantly, falling from 39.2 to 28.4 (Exhibit 8). This suggests shippers are seeing fewer trucks available relative to 
demand than in the previous survey. 
According to BofA Global Research, the current trucking upcycle is being driven by constrained supply rather than a significant 
improvement in freight demand. While any positive movement in demand is an important indicator as the industry moves into 
peak shipping season between late September and early December, tighter capacity appears to be the dominant force 
supporting freight rates. 
Exhibit 8: Shippers’ view of available capacity has fallen 
significantly as of October 1, 2026 
BofA Truckload Capacity Indicator by year (bi-weekly)

months after 4.5 months above 
BofA Truckload Demand Indicator (bi-weekly)

Source: BofA Global Research  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The reduction in capacity reflects several factors, including stricter commercial driver's license (CDL) and training requirements, 
increased compliance and enforcement measures, and other regulatory changes that have reduced the pool of qualified drivers. 
This has also likely driven some wage inflation within the industry. According to Bank of America payments data, transportation 
and warehousing payroll growth per client has surged since the start of the year, especially for mid-sized businesses (Exhibit 10).  
Having to pay higher wages suggests labor shortages remain a challenge in the sector. Firms may increasingly need to offer 
higher compensation to attract and retain workers as trucking capacity tightens. In turn, such capacity puts upward pressure on 
freight rates, particularly in the spot market where small businesses may be more impacted. 
Exhibit 10: Transportation and warehousing payroll growth has surged since the start of the year, especially for mid-sized businesses

Logistics payments tend to increase in the second half of the year 
September marks a key run-up to the holiday season (read more on signs of holiday spending in the October Consumer 
Checkpoint), with spending on ocean shipping, containers, and other transportation services steadily accelerating ahead of the 
peak shopping period.  
In fact, over the past two years, the month of November has seen ocean shipping/container spending volumes well above their 
2023-2025 monthly average (Exhibit 11). The seasonal pattern likely reflects inventory restocking and holiday-related shipping 
activity. Ocean shipping volumes typically accelerate during the fall months as businesses prepare for year-end demand. 
Exhibit 11: Ocean shipping/container spending volumes were well above their 2023-2025 monthly average in November 
Logistics and freight average payments by category (monthly, indexed, 100 = monthly 2023-2025 average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Distributors and inventory payments’ growth fell ahead of holiday season 
Changes in logistics and freight spending often provide an early signal of inventory trends, as distributors account for a large 
share of supply chain-related spending (Exhibit 12). 
The sharp moderation in distribution and inventory spending growth per client for both small and mid-sized businesses indicates 
that much of the large-scale inventory accumulation seen earlier in 2025 has already occurred (Exhibit 13). In other words, 
businesses appear to be replenishing inventories for seasonal demand rather than significantly expanding stockpiles as they did 
earlier in the year. 
Overall, businesses seem to be entering the holiday season in relatively stable financial condition, supported by tariff refunds 
and positive profitability growth. However, rising logistics costs, tighter trucking capacity and elevated supply chain pressures 
suggest operating conditions are becoming more challenging.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business and/or Business Banking clients with a deposit account or a 
Small Business credit card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of 
America per Business client data represents activity spending from active Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card and at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and are 
generally defined as under $5mm in annual sales revenue. Mid-sized businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America 
and are generally defined as $5mm-$50mm in annual sales revenue. 
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Revenue tiers are determined by the combination of following factors: 1) stated revenue on small businesses credit applications, 2) actual 
account inflow into Bank of America Deposit Accounts, and 3) third party revenue estimation.  
The alternative hiring indicator consists of payments from Bank of America business clients to business-focused hiring firms which include both 
direct deposits through Automated Clearing House (ACH) and payments via credit and debit cards. 
Supply chain-related spending is comprised of payments to logistics, freight, distribution, inventory, delivery service, and trucking companies. 
These are identified from merchant names and MCC codes. 
BofA Global Research’s trucking indicators are a bi-weekly diffusion-style survey of shipper demand expectations over the next 0–3 months, 
built from responses from surveyed shippers. 
Data regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

FINANCIAL CONDITIONS IN THE EUROZONE: A TIGHTENING TEMPORARILY TEMPERED BY CREDIT VOLUMES
Thomas Humblot
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
Since June 2026, the ECB has been publishing 1 a new indicator that helps to assess the transmission of its monetary policy to the economy:
the Broad Intermediation Gauge 2 (ECB-BIG). This new tool has revealed that, thus far, strong credit growth has limited the restrictive impact

of tightening financial conditions caused by rising interest rates in the Eurozone. However, this offsetting effect is beginning to fade.
The ECB-BIG: a new indicator for a broader view of financial conditions
The ECB-BIG complements the range of indicators used by the European Central Bank (ECB) to assess the effective transmission of its monetary
policy. This new tool provides a comprehensive view of how restrictive (or expansive) financial conditions are in the Eurozone. It stands out

for its approach that focuses more specifically on intermediation conditions. Therefore, the ECB-BIG provides a more comprehensive view than
most other financial condition indicators of financial conditions. In fact, it covers variables relating to the volume of bank lending to households
and businesses (the ‘volume’ component). Traditionally, this type of indicator tends to focus more on market variables (primarily interest and
exchange rates), share prices and corporate spreads. In addition, the ‘price’ component of the ECB-BIG is extended to include the cost of bank

loans, which is another new feature.
The rise in interest rates was temporarily offset by increased lending
By aggregating the variables relating to banking and non-banking intermediation conditions, the ECB-BIG shows that rising interest rates
(negative price effect) were offset in particular by lending volumes, which have continued to rise in 2026 (positive volume effect). A more
‘traditional’ indicator of financial conditions, the Macro-Finance FCI (MF-FCI) — which is, incidentally, included in the ECB-BIG — reveals a

much more pronounced tightening over the same period (see the orange curve in the chart). However, this offsetting of the price effect by the
volume effect is now beginning to wane, a sign that monetary policy is being effectively transmitted to the economy. Financial intermediaries
are gradually passing on the rise in rates to their customers (see the dotted green lines, which represent the composite indicators of the cost
of new loans to households and businesses, combining interest rates for different maturities), while lending volumes are falling. As a result,

between July and August 2026, seasonally adjusted flows of loans to households and businesses almost halved.
1 P . Lane, 2026, The Outlook for the Euro Area Economy, Background slides for Reuters NEXT Europe, London, 16 June 2026
2 A. Allayioti et al., 2026, The ECB-BIG index: tracking credit conditions in the euro area, ECB Economic Bulletin, Issue 5/2026
SOURCE: ALLAYIOTI AND AL. (2026), ECB, BLETZINGER ET AL. (2026), BNP PARIBAS ESTIMATES

In the longer term, financial conditions are expected to tighten, including as measured by the ECB-BIG
Despite the very sharp widening of sovereign spreads between 28 September and 2 October, financial conditions as measured by the
MF-FCI did not tighten, with sovereign yields actually falling over this period (with the exception of the OAT and the BTP), for example.

Nevertheless, the high volatility of the bond market, particularly in the sovereign segment, is likely to continue to affect financial
conditions. Looking slightly further ahead, the ECB’s key interest rate rises — the one that occurred on 16 September and another that we
anticipate for December 2026 (both +25 bp) — are likely to result in a more pronounced tightening. With a time lag of a number of weeks,
financial conditions, as defined by the ECB-BIG, are also expected to tighten. The price effect is likely to lead to a negative volume effect

on this occasion.

Document 295: France - 2027 Budget_ Fiscal consolidation must now resume (2026-10-01)

### Page 1

Paris, 1st October 2026

EcoFlash

2027 Budget: Fiscal consolidation must now resume

The 2027 budget, adopted by the French Council of Ministers today, calls for a reduction in France’s fiscal 
deficit to 5% of GDP in 2027 (down from 5.4% in 2026). This is a first step toward s the multi-year fiscal 
consolidation needed to stabilise the public debt-to-GDP ratio. This ratio is expected to rise further in 2027 
and until the deficit is reduced to 3% of GDP. Furthermore, given the increase in some expenditures (+0.6 pp 
of GDP for debt service and defence spending), the projected fiscal effort amounts to 1 pp. Nearly one-third 
of this effort would come from higher compulsory levies, and two -thirds from curbing government and 
social spending.

2027 Draft finance law: A step in the right direction. The government is forecasting an improvement in the fiscal 
deficit from 5.4% of GDP in 2026 to 5% in 2027. This projection is based on a credible growth assumption of 1% 
(see our analysis of French growth drivers). To achieve this target, compulsory levies (CL) are expected to rise to 
44.2% of GDP, an increase of 0.3 pp (the same increase as in 2026, compared with +0.9 pp in 2025). The public 
spending-to-GDP ratio would decline by 0.2 pp to 56.9%. The effort required to reduce the deficit to 5% is greater 
than it appears. Indeed, some expenditures will increase (interest payments by 0.4 pp and defence spending by 
0.2 pp). Consequently, the fiscal effort for next year amounts not to 0.4 pp, but to 1 pp of GDP. One-third of this will 
be achieved through increased CL, and two-thirds through spending cuts. Social spending will contribute, notably 
through the cancellation of EUR 3 billion in social contribution reductions for low-wage earners, as well as efforts 
totaling EUR 6 billion each in the areas of pensions and healthcare. Social spending shows the largest deviation 
from pre-COVID levels, with an increase representing 1.2 pp of GDP, nearly half of the gap between the 2026 
budget balance and a 3% deficit. Government spending, excluding interest payments and defence, is expected to 
be frozen in nominal terms.

A compromise seems likely despite a tight schedule. The French National Assembly will start reviewing the 
budget on Tuesday 13 October. The government will need to ensure that a majority vote against the draft budget 
does not form. The current circumstances, including increasing sovereign bond yields and the upcoming 
presidential election, call for a consensus based on fiscal consolidation of the size proposed by the government. If 
Parliament does not pass a budget by the end of the year, the government will have to choose between 
implementing the government’s budget proposal by executive order ( only possible if there was no parliamentary 
vote) or through a special law (as in 2025 and 2026). A special law would enable tax collection , while the 
government and social security agencies could continue to issue debt. In this case, the 2026 budget would apply 
in 2027 (without indexation) until Parliament passes a budget in the first few weeks of the year. This must occur 
before the end of February (when Parliament’s session ends, earlier than usual due to the presidential election).

The one-year postponement of the 5% budget deficit target has a measurable impact. The gap between the 
budget adopted for 2026 (deficit of 5% of GDP) and the actual budget outcome (deficit of 5.4%) is due to 
unforeseeable circumstances (the conflict in Iran  and extreme drought). However, postponing the deficit target  
previously set for 2026 to 2027 comes at a cost: 1) the public debt-to-GDP ratio is expected to reach 121% in 2027 
according to our forecasts (1 pp above our scenario with a 5% target in 2026 and a deficit reduced to 4.5% in 2027), 
and 2) the (upcoming) postponement to 2032 of the 3% of GDP deficit target (a commitment that most presidential 
candidates plan to make) rather than in 2029 (the government had committed to this with the European Commission 
in 2024). In 2032, the public debt-to-GDP ratio would stabilise at 124% of GDP (instead of 121.5% if the deficit had 
been reduced to 5% of GDP as early as 2026 and to 3% of GDP by the end of the decade).

Another delay in fiscal consolidation would further erode France’s credibility. This new delay could stem 
either from a decision by the government formed in May 2027 to immediately implement part of its agenda, or from 
the enactment of a special law that would remain in effect until (just after) the likely legislative elections in June  
2027  or  for  the  entire  year.  If  the  government  emerging  from  the  elections  were  to  resume  fiscal  
consolidation only in 2028 (with a deficit of 5% of GDP that it projects to reduce to 3% by 2032), public debt would 
reach 126% of GDP by that date (a one-year delay in fiscal consolidation would result in an additional 2 pp of 
debt). This further delay could potentially undermine the credibility of the next government, given that budget 
targets were  not met in three out of four years between 2023 and 2026, a situation that would be costly amid a 
sharp rise in long-term interest rates. Furthermore, a higher deficit would increase the amount of debt to be issued 
within an already  tight schedule. The Agence France Trésor (AFT) has announced debt issuances totaling EUR 
340 billion in 2027 (an  increase  of  EUR  20  billion  compared  to  2026).  This  increase  is  due  to  the  maturing  
COVID  related  debt (beginning in 2027 and continuing largely through 2030), while the amount of debt to be 
issued to finance the deficit will remain stable compared to 2026. A decrease from this last driver would be needed 
in subsequent years so that  the AFT’s debt issuance can stabilise.

Stéphane Colliac 
Head of the Advanced Economies Team, France 
stephane.colliac@bnpparibas.com

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS)

We expect another slight increase in the 
SGD NEER policy band slope at the MAS’ 
October review, with no change to the 
band’s width or centre. Import cost 
pressures and resilient growth favour a 
calibrated tightening, rather than a more 
forceful adjustment, in line with the 
global monetary policy stance.

Also due to be released alongside the 
MAS’ policy decision are the 3Q26 
advance GDP estimates, which we 
expect to show another quarter of strong 
growth at 5.4% yoy (1.4% qoq sa), 
compared with 5.9% yoy (1.4% qoq sa) in 
2Q26. Robust economic performance 
continued to be driven by artificial 
intelligence ( AI)-led trade activity, 
supporting the manufacturing and 
wholesale trade sectors, while strength 
in the financial sector underpinned the 
resilience of the modern services cluster. 
This came despite a moderation of the 
transport & storage sector, and 
weakness in consumer -facing food 
services and retail sectors.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Malaysia

We expect Malaysia’s advance GDP 
estimate for 3Q26 to register above -
trend growth of 5.0% yoy, albeit 
moderating from 6.0% yoy  in 2Q26. The 
overall expansion continued to be 
supported by ongoing strength in the 
export-oriented manufacturing sector, 
underpinned by strong  AI-related 
demand for electronics, and sustained 
domestic demand, backed by healthy 
investment and private consumption 
trends. However, contraction in mining 
and weak agricultural activity dragged 
overall growth.

Singapore

We expect firm Singapore non -oil 
domestic exports (NODX) growth of 28.0% 
yoy in September 2026, although 
moderating from August’s exceptional 
surge of 46.2%. Although favourable 
base effects largely faded in September, 
electronics shipments likely remained 
the primary growth driver, underpinned 
by robust AI -related demand amid 
sustained global investment in AI 
infrastructure, while non-electronics 
exports were uneven.

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 9, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

China

Exports growth is expected to remain 
robust at 25.7% y oy in September, 
supported by continued strength in 
electronics demand. Both the official and 
Caixin manufacturing PMIs rose above 
the 50 expansion threshold during the 
month, pointing to improving 
manufacturing activity. External trade 
indicators also stren gthened, with 
average daily deadweight tonnage 
handled at China's 20 major ports 
accelerating from -0.7% yoy in August to 
7.2% in September. Freight activity 
improved as well, as growth in 
international cargo flights picked up 
from -1.4% y oy to 3.6% over the same 
period.

On the price front, factory -gate and raw 
material prices rebounded amid the 
renewed escalation in geopolitical 
tensions. Main raw material purchasing 
price and producer price subPMI 
increased from 56.6 and 50.4 to 60.8 and 
54.0 during the period. However, 
downstream consumer price pressures 
are likely to remain relatively subdued 
under the pricing-band mechanism. As a 
result, CPI inflation is expected to edge 
up from 0.8% y oy in August to 1.0% in 
September. Nevertheless, soft domestic 
demand is expected to continue 
constraining the pace of consumer price 
increases.

India

September inflation is likely to gather 
momentum to 5.7% y/y, partly driven by 
base effects, from 4.8% the month 
before. Food segments continue to 
register gains, with pressures 
broadening to include perishables, 
edible oils, rice, pulses, sugar, etc., 
according to high-frequency data. Under 
non-food, retail pump prices have not 
been adjusted further, though other fuel 
products, such as non -subsidised LPG, 
diesel and ATF (still up by double digits 
y/y, though slower than in August), are 
up on the year. Core  inflation is also 
likely to tick up, aligning with the central 
bank's view that price risks are no longer 
benign. Pressures have built up due to 
the overhang of an uneven and sub -par 
monsoon, drought conditions in parts of 
the country, and elevated oil pr ices. In 
the face of higher headline prints, we 
expect the RBI to keep the door open for 
another rate hike in December 2026.

### Page 3

Past performance is not a reliable indicator of future performance. The forecasts given above are predictive in character. Whilst every effort has been taken to ensure that the assumptions on which the forecasts are based are reasonable, 
the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Markets have had little respite over the past month. Hopes 
of a lasting US–Iran agreement have faded and intermittent 
attacks on shipping have kept Brent oil prices in triple digits. 
Workarounds have allowed a meaningful share of crude to 
continue flowing but these arrangements are slower and 
costlier, while depleted inventories leave little room to 
absorb another shock and a range of disruptions to refining 
and shipping are also impacting downstream energy costs. 
Bond markets have been just as restless, with continued lifts 
in government bond yields across much of the globe and 
more sharply in the US. 
Notably, oil is no longer doing the heavy lifting for yields. 
The rise has been concentrated in real yields, with the Fed’s 
September hike – and its signal of more to come – acting as 
something of a trigger. In the US, fiscal concerns have been 
more of a sub-plot, though the Treasury’s efforts to hold 
down long yields have arguably only drawn more attention 
to them. 
In our view, markets are getting ahead of themselves on 
monetary policy offshore. We expect just one more hike 
from the Fed rather than the three the market currently has 
priced, with stretched housing affordability posing risks to 
both consumption and inflation. Central banks in Europe, the 
UK and Canada are also likely to bide their time after a final 
move. Europe is weathering the energy shock better than 
feared and has far more fiscal room to respond than the US – 
one reason we still see the US dollar trending lower over the 
next two years.
The RBA hiked again in September and looks set to follow 
up in November, taking the cash rate to a new cycle high. 
Some of the risks the Board flagged have crystallised, 
but the minutes also reveal a tendency to reach for the 
more hawkish read of the data, in addition to the familiar 
downbeat take on supply capacity. Our base case assumes 
no return to full-scale conflict, but with energy buffers 
depleted, bond markets less forgiving and the RBA already 
leaning hawkish, the margin for error – for markets and 
policymakers alike – is starting to ‘wear thin’.
Australia: After a soft but better than expected first half, the 
Australian economy is facing into more challenges as another 
round of disruptions from global energy markets combine with 
further interest rate rises and a deepening housing downturn. 
The demand mix should see capacity pressures continue to 
ease but with significant risks on either side.
Middle East: Low inventories and higher shipping costs will 
keep oil and fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast 
to average US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by 
Q4 2027 as supply recovers. Risks are two-sided: escalation 
could push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran 
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply.
Commodities: Most commodities rose in September, with 
many of the themes that drove moves across August remaining 
in place. Japanese LNG and Brent oil recorded strong gains 
amid Middle East tensions. Copper and aluminium extended 
recent gains while iron ore was supported by higher freight 
and production costs. Gold fell after the Fed’s September rate 
hike. 
Global FX Markets: In late September and early-October 
the DXY index popped higher to near 103, a high back to 
early-2025. Underlying the move are expectations that 
inflation will prove persistent globally as well as a belief that 
the US’ tech sector leaves it best positioned to weather the 
storm.
New Zealand: Higher fuel costs and a weaker exchange rate 
have lifted the short-term inflation outlook while the economy 
has continued to grow through the Iran war. Growth looks set 
to continue to be buoyed by strong commodity prices and a 
supportive exchange rate. However, downside risks are growing 
from higher energy costs and long-term interest rates. 
United States: The US housing market remains under 
considerable pressure. Prices continue to edge up, supporting 
wealth. But up-and-coming main street consumers are 
struggling with affordability and availability, posing enduring 
risks for both consumption growth and inflation.   
China: With each month that passes, the risk of disappointing 
authorities’ 4.5-5.0% ambition grows. While the Government 
is right to take a targeted approach to support, it must quickly 
multiply its scale if downside risks are to be kept at bay. 
Asia: For much of the past twenty years, Asia has been a 
source of disinflation for the world. But a combination of ageing 
populations, rising labour costs, increasing electricity demand 
and technology sector developments imply Asia may provide 
less support to global disinflation over the decade ahead.
Europe & UK: The euro has come under considerable pressure 
during the past month. That said, the European economy 
continues to tick along nicely while inflation risks remain 
manageable, allowing the ECB to be measured in their response.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
The main development affecting global markets this month
has been the sharp lift in selected bond yields, especially in
the US and France. Since the previous Market Outlook was
published, 10-year benchmarks have risen 27bps for US

Treasuries, to be well above the 5% threshold, and 40bps for
French bonds. The latter move contrasts with German bunds,
which have seen little change in yields at that tenor over the
month, though they are up more than 60bps year to date.
Several factors are behind the latest rise, with no single
cause dominating. For some of these factors, the underlying
conditions had been in evidence for some time, but recent
events focused attention on them. The rise has been primarily

a story of real yields: longer-run inflation expectations implied
by both breakevens and swaps have not increased that much.
For much of the year, fluctuations in oil prices – and thus the
near-term inflation outlook – fed into market views of central
bank policy, and longer yields as well. More recently, though,
this correlation has broken down. Shifts in expectations about
central bank policy have remained important for bond pricing,
as evident in the concentration of the increase in real yields.

The FOMC’s decision to increase the Fed funds rate, and flag in
the ‘dot plots’ that at least one more hike was likely, seems to
have been something of a trigger event for repricing, not only
for US yields. Also salient were the various actions taken by
the US Treasury to suppress long yields and improve the US
government’s debt-servicing position. These efforts had little
lasting effect and yields are now higher, and higher relative to
peers, than before. But they may have served to draw attention

to the growing issues around US fiscal sustainability. High
supply of debt from hyperscaler firms to fund the AI / data
centre boom has also likely pressured yields at the margin.
That said, fiscal concerns are unlikely to have been the main
issue driving the lift in US yields. The slope of the US yield
curve is noticeably flatter than at the beginning of the year;
in other words, short yields have risen even more than the
10-year yield, signifying tighter expected monetary policy

lifting yields across the curve. Another lens on this shift is that
2-year and other short-dated yields have increased more than
estimates of risk-neutral rate, such as that from the NY Fed.
Fiscal concerns have been more salient in the case of France,
where spreads to bunds have widened from around 70bps at
the beginning of the year to nearly 140bps currently, wider than
Greece or Italy, with about 40bps of the widening occurring in
the past month. Contagion to other euro area sovereigns has

been limited, with spreads widening around 22bps over the
month for Greece, Italy and Belgium, and 13bps for Spain.
Many factors lifting global bond yields ...
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FINANCIAL MARKETS
... and RBA policy rates outlook
In Australia, the RBA hiked the cash rate again at its late
September meeting, and a further hike at the November
meeting looks likely. This would take the cash rate to 4.85%, a
new cycle high.
While the forecasts the RBA published in August did not

support further rate hikes, in September these forecasts were
characterised as resting on several things going right and
certain risks not emerging. Some of these risks, including a re-
escalation of the war in the Middle East and thus energy prices,
have since crystallised. The post-meeting communication
and minutes also pointed to the July CPI result being above
expectations, as was June quarter GDP growth “at the margin”.
The RBA is also wary about pressure on both construction

costs and retail prices of IT goods arising from the AI/data
centre boom. No weight is put on the possibility that this new
investment and technology might add to productivity or the
capital stock, and so supply capacity, even with a lag. Indeed,
the post-meeting minutes highlighted the financial stability
risks if valuations in this area should disappoint “even if the
technology were ultimately successful”, as if the latter was not
yet clear. This language points to a generally downbeat view of

Australian supply capacity that also emerges in its discussion
of productivity trends. Although its position piece in the August
2025 Statement on Monetary Policy  indicated that lower trend
growth in productivity had no implications for inflation or
monetary policy, more recent statements imply that it does.
More broadly, the minutes for the September meeting revealed
a tendency to contemplate more hawkish interpretations
of data and outcomes. For example, the minutes dismissed

evidence of the labour market easing a bit more than
was forecast by pointing to the changes to the survey
arrangements. At the same time, the minutes contemplated
that the rate of unemployment consistent with inflation
being at target might have risen, even though wages growth
has marginally undershot the RBA’s forecasts and the cost
pressures cited by its business liaison contacts are mostly
non-labour costs. The minutes also included an extended

discussion of the possibility that the global neutral interest
rate might have increased recently. If true, this would imply
that financial conditions were less restrictive. This conclusion
would be hard to square with yield curves that have flattened
since the beginning of the year and other central bank
estimates of the term premium having increased, both of which
suggest tighter financial conditions.
That said, the RBA will find it hard to look past inflation

outcomes in the second half of year that is likely to remain
sticky. We anticipate trimmed mean inflation to record
quarterly outcomes of around 0.9%qtr in Q3 and Q4, remaining
around the current 3½%yr pulse. The Monetary Policy Board
will want to see clear evidence of inflation coming down before
withdrawing policy restrictiveness, so we continue to pencil in
August as the likely date for start of the rate-cutting phase.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
After posting a soft but better than expected first half, the
Australian economy is facing into more challenges near term
as another round of disruptions from global energy markets
combine with a further rise in interest rates and a deepening

housing downturn. Against this, business investment is in
the midst of a sustained ramp-up led by a large and growing
pipeline of data centre and energy investment projects.
Elsewhere, public demand is growing at a slower pace and
new dwelling investment is likely to moderate but both are
expected to still see positive growth. Overall, the demand
mix should see capacity pressures continue to ease but with
significant risks on either side.
Shifting political winds

Some of these risks are now in the wider policy space. The
political landscape has changed materially since the 2025
Federal Election with opinion polls showing a sustained swing
against the Labor government and a marked rise in support
for One Nation that raises the prospect of a three-horse race
in the next election, the outcome of which could be very hard
to predict (we may get a preview of this with next month’s
Victorian state election). While the next Federal election is

still a long way off – not due until well into 2028 – the shift
does appear to be having some bearing on policy. For example,
the government’s choice not to reinstate the temporary
halving in fuel excise tax that was deployed when fuel prices
first spiked back in March suggests it is less inclined towards
providing broad based cost-of-living support that might make
it harder to bring inflation back down.
Migration policy is shaping more clearly as a battleground.

A suite of changes announced by the government in mid-
September and which came into effect in October, are already
expected to slow working holidaymaker and foreign student
arrivals, moderating total net overseas migration from
around 300k current to 225k by 2028, trimming population
growth from around 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr. For its part, the main
Liberal-National opposition party has proposed to reduce net
migration to 100k in the first two years of government, before

increasing to 130k and 160k in the two years following –
which would likely see total population growth slow to around
0.7-0.8%yr initially. One Nation has proposed a period of ‘net
negative’ migration, lowering net migration by 250k a year
over the first three years of government before considering a
‘ceiling’ of 130k per year thereafter. This would see population
growth slow to around 0.2-0.3%yr initially.
While none of these policies are on the table for this year

or next, the direction of travel is clear with a risk that the
current government moves further towards curbing migrant
More challenges near term ...
Matthew Hassan, Head of Australian Macro-Forecasting
inflows. Slower population growth basically maps one-to-
one to GDP growth although the specific flow-on impacts to
areas like housing and labour markets are more complex and
will depend on the population mix and how the demand and
supply effects balance out. For the record, we have trimmed

our own population growth forecasts in the 2028 out-year,
from 1.4%yr to 1.2%yr with a related tweak to our GDP
growth forecast for that year.
Consumer sentiment-spending gap
Near term challenges continue to centre on the consumer.
Here, the latest updates show sentiment again plumbing
historical lows but more mixed messages around spending
which is still holding up better than might be expected based
on a simple read on the consumer mood.
Certainly that mood remains deeply negative. The Westpac–

Melbourne Institute Consumer Sentiment Index dropped
4.7% to 80.4 in October, putting the month amongst the forty
worst reads since the began in the early 1970s, following
two others reads in this group back in April and June and
another eight recorded in 2022–23. Altogether this marks the
worst period of recurring extremely weak sentiment since the
disastrous recession in the early 1990s.
This has been difficult to square with the soft rather than

outright weak picture on consumer spending through most
of this year. Even when real spending was recording more
material per capita declines back in 2023-24, these were
somewhat milder than what might have been expected given
the weakness in sentiment at the time.
We explored this more fully in our August Red Book . Some
of the disconnect may be a by-product of high inflation which
‘hits different’ sentiment-wise. Cost of living pressures,

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUSTRALIAN ECONOMY
... centred on uneven consumer, housing downturn ...
which are the central issue for consumers at the moment,
represent a broad-based but shallower squeeze rather than
the deeper shocks that typically come when big economic
downturns hit. Labour markets appear to be an important

point of distinction – despite the weakness in sentiment,
consumers have been relatively unperturbed about the jobs
outlook in recent years. Adding consumers’ unemployment
expectations to sentiment measures significantly improves its
‘fit’ as a predictor of per capita spending.
There other aspect that helps to reconcile the sentiment-
spending gap is the detailed picture across segments. There
may be one economy but there is not a single consumer.

Instead both sentiment and spending outcomes reflect the
average across a range of often quite varied conditions in
different consumer segments.
This shows through in the dispersion of sentiment across
sub-groups. The chart right shows the current distribution of
index reads across the 100-odd sub-groups we regularly track
with some selected groups called out. If the range seems wide
its because it is – measures of spread over the last two years
have been 50-60% wider than the average over the previous

three decades. Exactly why is not completely clear but the
observations point to a more fractured consumer with multiple
fault lines – including across states, metro/regional areas, voter
groups, labour force status, income levels and housing tenures.
Spending-wise, one of the clearest emerging fault lines is across
age groups. Here, there look to be three main ‘blocs’ – 18-34 year
olds (mainly millennials’); 35-54 year olds (mainly ‘gen X’); and

those aged over 55 (dominated by the ‘baby boomers’).
Demographically, the 55+ age group is both the largest and
the fastest-growing – our estimates suggest it now makes up
about 45% of all households, up 8.5pts compared to 2020. It
is also seeing significant financial shifts with the cohort coming
through carrying materially larger superannuation holdings
(reflecting a variety of factors including a longer period of
compulsory contributions that have earned compounding

returns) and more housing wealth. Again, our rough estimates
suggest average household wealth in this group is around
$2.5m up over $800k since 2020 (reflecting both the
aforementioned demographic shifts and valuation changes).
At the same time, the group has low debt levels – it is largely
untouched by interest rate increases and probably benefits
slightly insofar as this lifts interest income both directly and
indirectly via super holdings.
For 18-34 year olds, incomes are more important than balance

sheets, meaning inflation poses more of a constraint. For 35-
54 year olds, income is less of a constraint but high debt levels
poses a big challenge as rate rises increase servicing costs.
All of which is to say that it should come as little surprise that
spending by the 55+ age group has been running stronger
than the other age groups. Breakdowns from our Westpac
Consumer Panel show average non fuel spend has been rising
at a 5% annual pace over the last six months amongst those

aged 55+. That compares to a more sedate 2.7-2.9% pace 
across the other age groups, which given the current pace of 
inflation, implies small declines in real, inflation-adjusted terms.
These demographic contrasts also account for the observed 
strength in key bellwether spending categories like new 
vehicle purchases. The total number of new car purchases 
by consumers is up close to 4%yr, led by strong growth in 
EVs. Notably, industry figures suggest the purchase of used 
cars is down sharply, by close to 9%yr (used car sales are 
not well covered in the statistics and largely bypass official 
ABS measures because the are mainly transfers between 
households rather than a new addition). 
Pulling these clues together, at least some of the resilience in 
spending looks to be a form of ‘wealth effect’ where balance 
sheet considerations are more important than the real purchasing 
power of incomes. While the impact of employment, wages, 
inflation and interest rate changes will still likely be the dominant 
driver of shifts in spending growth, the nature of this wealth 
effect and how it evolves from here could be a key swing factor. 
Housing downturn deepens
And where that swing factor may start to turn is in response 
to a deepening downturn in Australia’s housing market. The 
combined impact of interest rate rises and major changes to 
housing-related tax policy announced in the Federal budget is 
continuing to weigh heavily. We have been flagging for some 
time that uncertainty could see a more significant ‘air pocket’ 
emerge with very thin trading conditions and a sharper move 
lower in prices. That risk is crystallising with turnover already 
down 20% and prices off 8%. 
With the September interest rate rise and an expected hike 
in November still to come, more weakness is likely near term. 
We revised our house price forecasts lower with a decline of 
around 8% now expected for calendar 2026, with a peak-to-
trough decline of just over 10%. 
... wealth effects an uncertain swing factor 
This constitutes a more material housing downturn. The 
peak to trough fall is set to be biggest since the beginning of 
the national price measures in 1980, although prices in real, 
inflation-adjusted terms recorded larger falls during the early-
1990s and early-1980s recessions with capital city measures 
also suggesting there were larger real price falls during the 
recessions in the mid-1970s and early 1960s. 
It also points to a bigger housing-related drag on growth. 
As discussed previously, the main channels are through 
new dwelling investment, turnover and wealth effects. New 
dwelling investment is still carrying solid momentum (the 
number of dwelling under construction up 10% over the year 
to June) but is expected to see a slightly more pronounced 
slowing. Turnover is also expected to be slightly weaker. 
However, it is the wealth effects from price declines that is the 
area to watch. Our rough estimates suggest this will account 
for most of the –0.8ppts on annual GDP growth over the 
year to June 2027. The emphasis here though is on ‘rough’. 
Wealth effects have seeming disappeared for long periods 
and their precise timing is very unclear, e.g. how should the 
drag from the current downturn be viewed against what 
were presumably strong positives coming off the much larger 
preceding gains? Some of this could be due to ‘assymmetric’ 
behaviour, with effects playing out very differently when there 
wealth is rising or falling.
On balance, we expect the impact to see consumer spending 
‘muddle through’ rather than fall back to the weaker growth 
seen during the more intense cost of living and interest 
rate shocks in 2023-24. This partly reflects the indirect 
support coming from growth more generally – the AI-related 
investment boom, which we have covered at length in previous 
reports, a key factor helping to hold GDP growth up around 
1.5%yr and prevent a more material weakening in labour 
market conditions. For the consumer though this will continue 
to feel like a struggle with growth flat in per capita terms 
and a shakier picture around housing that will play out very 
differently for some segments.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
MIDDLE EAST
Low inventories and higher shipping costs will keep oil and
fuel prices elevated, with Brent futures forecast to average
US$101/bbl in Q4 before easing to US$77/bbl by Q4 2027
as supply recovers. Refined fuels and LNG prices should
ease more slowly. Risks are two-sided: escalation could

push Brent towards US$150/bbl, while an early US-Iran
agreement could see a faster normalisation in supply with
prices returning close to pre-conflict levels by end-2027.
Brent crude is back above US$100/bbl on fading confidence
in a lasting US-Iran agreement and intermittent attacks on
shipping. Buffers remain depleted, with the IEA estimating
global observed inventories were 507mb lower at the end of
August than at the start of the conflict.
This comes despite crude exports through the Strait of Hormuz

recovering to around 70% to 90% of pre-conflict levels in late
September. Saudi and UAE exports through the Yanbu and
Fujairah pipelines continue, with the East-West pipeline back
to normal after the early September attacks. Tanker crossings
have risen, although transponders remain switched off, leaving
visible transit at 1% to 4% of pre-conflict levels, while cargo
transfers in the Gulf of Oman have increased.
However, these workarounds are slower and more costly.

Ship-to-ship transfers can take 5 to 10 days, US escorts cause
delays and some tankers are taking longer routes. Fewer ships
are also available, with many redeployed to other markets.
Higher insurance premiums are adding to costs, with Gulf-to-
China tanker rates above US$0.9mn a day, around 15x higher
than a year ago. With prompt barrels scarce, the gap between
physical and futures prices widened again, with Dated Brent
around US$136/bbl in early October against US$101/bbl for

front-month futures.
Oil and fuel prices push higher ...
Sian Fenner, Head of Business & Industry Economics
Justin Smirk, Senior Economist
Luka Belobajdic, Economist
Refined prices have risen more than crude
Refined product markets were already tight going into the
conflict. Reduced Gulf supply, exacerbated by Ukrainian strikes
on Russian refineries, has added to that tightness. Together
with higher shipping costs and rising Dated Brent prices,
Singapore Gasoil 10ppm (diesel) has roughly doubled since the

Strait closed, compared with around 40% for crude futures.
Consequently, Singapore gasoil crack spread widened to
an average of around US$36.1/bbl in early October, from
US$17.5/bbl across 2025. Alongside the government’s excise
tax cut returning to normal, this has lifted national diesel and
petrol prices to around A$2.83/L and A$2.41/L.
Outlook
We do not see a lasting US-Iran agreement until at least
mid-2027. In the meantime, we assume there will not be a

return to full-scale military intervention. However, periodic
Iranian attacks on ships and facilities are expected to continue,
as will the US blockade of Iranian exports, keeping a supply
risk premium in place. We look for Brent futures to average
around US$101/bbl in Q4, then ease gradually as demand
from refiners and governments to rebuild stocks partly offsets
recovering supply. As the premium fades and the market
moves into surplus in late 2027, we forecast Brent at around

US$77/bbl in Q4 2027, easing towards US$70/bbl in 2028 as
Middle East production fully recovers.
Oil balance to gradually improve over 2027
Global oil supply is expected to rise by 4.0mb/d (4.0%) to
103.7mb/d in 2027, following a 6.1% fall in 2026. Most of this
reflects a gradual recovery in Gulf output, with Houthi attacks
on Red Sea shipping keeping Saudi production below its
